Here, **graph engineering** means engineering graph-structured **AI-agent systems**. Graph databases, knowledge graphs, graph ETL, GraphRAG, and graph neural networks are outside this scope unless they directly support an agent graph. See the [boundary guide](COMPARISON.md#not-graph-data-engineering-the-name-collision).

## 🧭 Working definition

**Graph engineering is the practice of specifying, executing, observing, and evolving a graph-structured agent system—its roles and runtime instances, the contracts that connect them, the state and artifacts they share, and the evidence by which their collective behavior is judged—so that the system can be controlled, tested, and improved as an engineered whole.**

The graph must be load-bearing rather than decorative: its declared topology, realized run graph, or graph-generating policy materially constrains execution and remains inspectable enough to version, trace, evaluate, or deliberately change.

Connected improvement loops can monitor, constrain, or veto one another, while external measurements, fixed rules, and human decisions provide grounding. [Carlos E. Perez’s 2026 “graph of loops” essay](https://x.com/IntuitMachine/status/2078419526354378975) articulates this control perspective. Applied to AI-agent organizations, agent roles and runtime instances are the primary adaptive nodes; deterministic tests, audit cycles, human decisions, and real-world observations may act as control nodes or gates.

*Graph engineering* is used here as an **emerging, non-standard term**. Its scope synthesizes established work in [agent-oriented programming](https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(93)90034-9), [multi-agent systems](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0269888900008122), [blackboard architectures](https://doi.org/10.1609/aimag.v7i2.537), distributed systems, workflow orchestration, and graph-based language-agent research such as [GPTSwarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.16823). The [evidence map](DEFINITION.md#evidence-map-for-the-synthesis) separates source-backed claims from analytical inferences. Steinberger’s and Perez’s July 2026 posts document recent practitioner usage; they do not establish coinage or consensus.

### The minimum test

A system is in scope when all three conditions are load-bearing:

1. **Plural bounded agency** — at least two separately accountable agentic runtime instances each make bounded execution decisions; they may share a role or model.
2. **Explicit coordination semantics** — edges say what may move, when control transfers, and how results are accepted or rejected.
3. **An inspectable graph artifact** — topology, run graph, or graph-generating policy can be versioned, traced, evaluated, or changed deliberately.

A single agent with many tools is still one node. A deterministic DAG of ordinary functions is workflow engineering. A “swarm” of personas without contracts, state boundaries, or evidence gates is branding, not graph engineering.

## 🧩 Core primitives

| Primitive | Engineering question | Typical artifact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Agent node** | Who owns this context, objective, capability, and permission boundary? | Role spec, runtime identity, tool allowlist |
| **Typed edge** | What crosses this relationship, under which preconditions and schema? | Handoff contract, protocol message, artifact reference |
| **Org graph** | Which reusable roles may coordinate, delegate, verify, or escalate? | Versioned roles-and-permissions topology |
| **Run/work graph** | What execution structure does this particular job require? | Run-scoped DAG, event graph, trace, lineage |
| **Gate** | What evidence allows work to advance? | Test, evaluator, quorum rule, human approval |
| **State boundary** | What is shared, isolated, checkpointed, or authoritative? | Blackboard, artifact store, snapshot, worktree |
| **Graph policy** | Who may create, rewrite, cancel, or route nodes and edges? | Scheduler, budget policy, topology generator |

### Org graph ≠ run graph

| | Org graph | Run/work graph |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Describes** | Stable roles, capabilities, authority, and allowed relationships | The tasks, dependencies, branches, and evidence produced in one run |
| **Changes** | Deliberately, through architecture or policy updates | Dynamically, as planning and execution reveal new work |
| **Primary question** | “Who may do and verify what?” | “What must happen next for this outcome?” |
| **Safety boundary** | A planner may select permitted relationships | A planner must not silently expand its own permissions |

Org graph and run/work graph are analytical views, not standardized object types. An implementation may represent both in one runtime graph if standing authority remains distinguishable from run-specific execution.

## ✅ Do you need a graph?

Start with the smallest architecture that closes the quality loop. One well-instrumented agent loop is usually enough until work needs at least one of these:

- **Specialization:** independent roles need different context, tools, models, or permissions.
- **Parallelism:** separable work should fan out, then reconcile under an explicit contract.
- **Independent verification:** a producer should not be the only judge of its own output.
- **Fault or trust isolation:** failure, untrusted input, or privileged actions must be contained.

If none applies, invest in the loop first: context, stopping conditions, tests, retries, and observability. [Loop engineering](https://addyosmani.com/blog/loop-engineering/) is primarily temporal—how one agent progresses through repeated work. Graph engineering is primarily relational—how multiple scoped actors and control surfaces compose. The two concerns are complementary; neither is a maturity stage.

## 🧱 The nine engineering layers

<picture>
  <source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="assets/layers-map-dark.svg">
  <img src="assets/layers-map-light.svg" alt="Nine co-equal graph engineering design lenses: roles, topology, handoffs, work graphs, state, gates, reliability, observability and cost, and evolution">
</picture>

| # | Layer | The design question |
| ---: | --- | --- |
| 01 | **Roles** | Who exists, and what does each node own? |
| 02 | **Topology** | How are the roles arranged, and why this shape? |
| 03 | **Handoffs** | What may cross each edge, in what schema? |
| 04 | **Work graphs** | What execution structure does this run need now? |
| 05 | **State** | What is shared, isolated, durable, or authoritative? |
| 06 | **Gates** | What evidence advances, rejects, or escalates work? |
| 07 | **Reliability** | How does the graph fail, recover, replay, and stop? |
| 08 | **Observability & cost** | Can operators explain the path, latency, and spend? |
| 09 | **Evolution** | When and how may topology or policy redesign itself? |

The layers are concerns, not compulsory stages. A two-role pipeline with a typed handoff and a test gate can be a complete graph. Read the full [taxonomy](TAXONOMY.md), [comparison guide](COMPARISON.md), and [anti-patterns](ANTI-PATTERNS.md).

## 📚 Resource directory

The resource directory prioritizes primary research, official documentation, maintained projects, standards, reproducible benchmarks, and production reports. **Evidence labels identify source type; they do not score quality.** Each entry contains an original summary, a distinct engineering rationale, and one primary layer.

Concrete example of graph-shaped shared knowledge serving a standing agent organization rather than a retrieval pipeline.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A concrete early example of graph-shaped shared knowledge serving a standing agent team rather than a retrieval pipeline.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 📝 **[The Shift from Models to Compound AI Systems](https://bair.berkeley.edu/blog/2024/02/18/compound-ai-systems/)**<br><sub>Blog · Compound-systems economics</sub> | **Berkeley AI Research blog**<br><sub>Matei Zaharia, Omar Khattab, Lingjiao Chen et al. · 2024</sub> | The essay that reframed AI engineering as system design over budgets: tripling model training spend might buy 30%→35% on coding, while a sample-test-filter system reaches 80% with existing models, so builders must allocate latency, cost, and compute budgets across components. The economic argument underneath the whole engineering stack that graph engineering now sits atop.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Reframes AI engineering as system design over budgets, which is the premise the whole coordination layer rests on.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📝 **[Are we still talking loops or did we shift to graphs yet?](https://x.com/steipete/status/2078277297791189132)**<br><sub>Blog · Founding discourse</sub> | **X**<br><sub>Peter Steinberger · 2026</sub> | The July 2026 post that pushed the discourse from loop engineering to graph engineering: one line asking whether single-agent loops are already obsolete as the unit of design. Its virality reframed planning and decomposition as the question of what work graph your agents should form per task.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The post that named the shift from loops to graphs, and the entry point to the whole coordination discourse.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📝 **[Graph Engineering: Designing Multi-Agent Organizations](https://explainx.ai/blog/graph-engineering-ai-agents-multi-agent-organizations-2026)**<br><sub>Blog · Work graph vs org graph</sub> | **explainx.ai**<br><sub>Yash Thakker · 2026</sub> | Canonical framing article (July 18, 2026) separating the stable org graph of long-lived agents from the ephemeral work graph whose task nodes split, merge, reorder, or disappear as evidence arrives. Argues graphs force you to declare every node, edge, and failure mode that loops let you defer.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The framing article that separates the standing org graph from the per-run work graph, which is the field's most useful distinction.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 🧭 **[Awesome Harness Engineering](https://github.com/ai-boost/awesome-harness-engineering)**<br><sub>List · Adjacent curated lists</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>ai-boost · 2026</sub> | Curated resources for the harness layer directly beneath agent organizations: agent loops, context management, tool design, MCP and skills, permissions, memory, verification, observability, sandboxing, and reference implementations, at roughly 3.3k stars.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The neighbouring layer's catalog, useful when a graph problem turns out to be a single node's harness problem.</sub> | **Community resource**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |

Reports 65M+ monthly downloads.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The framework author's own account of what three years of building agent graphs taught them, including where the DAG mental model breaks.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[From Static Templates to Dynamic Runtime Graphs: A Survey of Workflow Optimization for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22386)**<br><sub>Paper · Field survey</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Ling Yue, Kushal Raj Bhandari, Ching-Yun Ko, Dhaval Patel, Shuxin Lin, Nianjun Zhou, Jianxi Gao, Pin-Yu Chen, Shaowu Pan · 2026</sub> | Surveys how agent systems build and optimize executable workflows, treating them as agentic computation graphs and separating reusable templates from the run-specific graph and its execution trace. Organizes the literature by when structure is fixed, what is optimized, and which signals guide it, and argues for structure-aware evaluation alongside task scores.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The closest thing to a field survey of this catalog's subject, and its template-versus-runtime-graph split matches the org and run graph distinction used throughout.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

### Research Foundations

| Resource | Source | What it contributes | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 📄 **[Agent-Oriented Programming](https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702%2893%2990034-9)**<br><sub>Paper · Agent foundations</sub> | **Artificial Intelligence**<br><sub>Yoav Shoham · 1993</sub> | Defines a programming paradigm in which agents are first-class components described through mental state and governed by explicit interaction rules.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Establishes the intellectual lineage for treating an agent role as a programmable organizational unit.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Intelligent Agents: Theory and Practice](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0269888900008122)**<br><sub>Paper · Agent foundations</sub> | **The Knowledge Engineering Review**<br><sub>Michael Wooldridge; Nicholas R. Jennings · 1995</sub> | Surveys the properties, architectures, and engineering approaches that distinguish autonomous agents from ordinary software modules.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Grounds the agency-at-the-nodes boundary that separates an agent graph from a deterministic workflow.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Blackboard Model of Problem Solving and the Evolution of Blackboard Architectures](https://doi.org/10.1609/aimag.v7i2.537)**<br><sub>Paper · Shared-state architectures</sub> | **AI Magazine**<br><sub>H. Penny Nii · 1986</sub> | Describes systems in which independent specialists coordinate opportunistically through a shared problem state and a control component.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Supplies a durable model for shared state without requiring every node to exchange its full context directly.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 📄 **[Learning to Communicate with Deep Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2016/hash/c7635bfd99248a2cdef8249ef7bfbef4-Abstract.html)**<br><sub>Paper · Learned communication</sub> | **NeurIPS**<br><sub>Jakob Foerster; Ioannis Alexandros Assael; Nando de Freitas; Shimon Whiteson · 2016</sub> | Introduces reinforcement-learning methods that let agents learn communication protocols alongside their task policies, including discrete messages for execution.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Shows that edge content and communication policy can be engineered or learned rather than treated as free-form chat.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[TarMAC: Targeted Multi-Agent Communication](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v97/das19a.html)**<br><sub>Paper · Learned communication</sub> | **ICML**<br><sub>Abhishek Das et al. · 2019</sub> | Uses attention to let agents address different messages to selected recipients instead of broadcasting the same information to the whole team.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Motivates selective, recipient-aware handoffs when all-to-all communication is wasteful or distracting.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[AutoGen: Enabling Next-Gen LLM Applications via Multi-Agent Conversations](https://openreview.net/forum?id=BAakY1hNKS)**<br><sub>Paper · LLM multi-agent systems</sub> | **COLM**<br><sub>Qingyun Wu; Gagan Bansal; Jieyu Zhang; Yiran Wu; Beibin Li; Erkang Zhu; Li Jiang; Xiaoyun Zhang; Shaokun Zhang; Jiale Liu; Ahmed Hassan Awadallah; Ryen W. White; Doug Burger; Chi Wang · 2024</sub> | Presents a framework for composing customizable conversational agents that can combine language models, tools, code execution, and human input.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> An early, influential demonstration that agent roles and conversation links can be expressed as an executable topology.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[GPTSwarm: Language Agents as Optimizable Graphs](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/zhuge24a.html)**<br><sub>Paper · Topology optimization</sub> | **ICML**<br><sub>Mingchen Zhuge et al. · 2024</sub> | Represents language-agent systems as computational graphs and optimizes graph components from task feedback.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Makes the graph itself an optimization target rather than a fixed orchestration diagram.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |
| 📄 **[A Dynamic LLM-Powered Agent Network for Task-Oriented Agent Collaboration](https://openreview.net/forum?id=XII0Wp1XA9)**<br><sub>Paper · Dynamic topology</sub> | **COLM**<br><sub>Zijun Liu et al. · 2024</sub> | Constructs task-specific collaboration networks that can vary which agents participate and how they communicate instead of relying on one fixed team.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Provides evidence for adapting the work graph to the task while keeping the available agent roles reusable.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |
| 📄 **[Cut the Crap: An Economical Communication Pipeline for LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://openreview.net/forum?id=LkzuPorQ5L)**<br><sub>Paper · Communication efficiency</sub> | **ICLR**<br><sub>Guibin Zhang, Yanwei Yue, et al. · 2025</sub> | First paper to formally define communication redundancy in LLM multi-agent pipelines, then one-shot prunes the spatial-temporal message-passing graph. Matches state-of-the-art topologies at $5.6 vs $43.7 cost, cuts 28-73% of tokens as a drop-in for existing frameworks, and incidentally defends against message-injection attacks — the canonical 'sparser edges are often free' result.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Defines communication redundancy formally, then removes it, which is the rare optimization that helps quality and cost together.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[G-Designer: Architecting Multi-agent Communication Topologies via Graph Neural Networks](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v267/zhang25cu.html)**<br><sub>Paper · Topology optimization</sub> | **ICML**<br><sub>Guibin Zhang et al. · 2025</sub> | Uses graph neural networks to design communication structures for multi-agent systems instead of assuming a complete or manually chosen graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Connects task performance to explicit topology search and exposes communication structure as an engineering variable.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |

| 📄 **[Too Polite to Disagree: Understanding Sycophancy Propagation in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02668)**<br><sub>Paper · Anti-groupthink gates</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>(see arXiv listing) · 2026</sub> | Documents how sycophancy propagates through agent organizations: instead of correcting each other, agents amplify each other's agreement patterns into collective conformity that entrenches errors. A 2026 empirical warning that stacking agreeable agents in a council produces confident consensus, not error correction — and why deliberation topologies need dissent mechanisms.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Documents sycophancy propagating through an organization, which is why agreement between nodes is weak evidence.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧰 **[llm-council: A Council of LLMs with a Chairman](https://github.com/karpathy/llm-council)**<br><sub>Tool · Council/committee design</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Andrej Karpathy · 2025</sub> | Karpathy's minimal working council: a query fans out to a fixed panel of frontier models (GPT-5.1, Gemini 3 Pro, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Grok 4 via OpenRouter), members review and rank each other's anonymized answers to prevent favoritism, and a Chairman synthesizes the final response. A weekend hack that became the reference implementation of committee-of-rivals topology with a built-in anti-bias gate.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A minimal working council with a chairman, useful as the smallest honest implementation of panel verification.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |

Scaled to 590 agents in a national-policy simulation and built a working Gobang game in ~800 seconds — an early concrete instance of the org graph rewriting itself on evidence.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Generating the org chart rather than writing it is the frontier claim, and this is a concrete attempt at it.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |

Representative of the newest wave: dependency inference as a learned capability rather than a prompt trick.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Inferring subtask dependencies as an explicit graph is what makes safe parallelism a derived property rather than a guess.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

| 📄 **[From Agent Loops to Structured Graphs: A Scheduler-Theoretic Framework for LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11378)**<br><sub>Paper · Loops vs graphs</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Hu Wei · 2026</sub> | April 2026 position paper applying classical scheduling theory to agent execution, diagnosing the agent loop's implicit step dependencies, unbounded recovery cycles, and mutable histories. Proposes a Structured Graph Harness with static DAG control flow, immutable plans, separated planning/recovery layers, and formal escalation, grounded in an analysis of 70 systems.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Applies scheduling theory to agent execution, diagnosing implicit dependencies and unbounded recovery precisely.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

The minimal ancestor of every plan-then-execute orchestrator prompt.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The minimal decompose-then-execute result, and the baseline any elaborate planner should be measured against.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

(SqueezeAILab, UC Berkeley) · 2024</sub> | ICML 2024 paper that treats the LLM as a compiler: a Planner emits a DAG of function-call tasks with placeholder variables, a Task Fetching Unit dispatches ready nodes in parallel, and an Executor runs them, unblocking dependents as results arrive. Reports up to 3.7x latency speedup, 6.7x cost savings, and ~9% higher accuracy than ReAct; the archetype of work-graph generation for tool calls (code: github.com/SqueezeAILab/LLMCompiler).<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Compiling a plan into a dependency graph of calls is the cleanest formulation of automatic parallelization.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

The variable-passing idea underlies most modern DAG planners including LLMCompiler.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Planning with placeholders separates reasoning from observation, which cuts token cost and makes plans inspectable.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

The standard bridge from the planning papers to working code, including why replanning beats one-shot plans and when small models can execute a big model's plan.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Walks through planning architectures including replanning, which is the part most implementations omit.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

Formalizes supervision trees and the worker/supervisor split that later gets borrowed for agent failure containment.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The thesis behind let-it-crash and supervision trees, the most battle-tested answer to what happens when a node dies.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📚 **[Erlang/OTP Supervisor Behaviour (Design Principles)](https://www.erlang.org/doc/system/sup_princ.html)**<br><sub>Docs · Actor model and supervision</sub> | **Erlang/OTP System Documentation**<br><sub>Ericsson / Erlang/OTP team · 2026</sub> | The operational spec for supervision trees: child specifications, the four restart strategies (one_for_one, one_for_all, rest_for_one, simple_one_for_one), and restart intensity limits (max restarts per time period) that stop a crash loop from cascading. Concrete prior art for how a parent node in an agent graph should react when a child dies.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Supervision strategies specified concretely, including restart policy, which is what an agent orchestrator eventually reimplements.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📐 **[FIPA Contract Net Interaction Protocol Specification (SC00029H)](http://www.fipa.org/specs/fipa00029/SC00029H.html)**<br><sub>Standard · Delegation and task allocation protocols</sub> | **Foundation for Intelligent Physical Agents**<br><sub>FIPA · 2002</sub> | Standardizes Smith's contract net as a message sequence with explicit deadlines and cancellation: cfp, propose or refuse, accept-proposal or reject-proposal, then inform-done, inform-result, or failure. A wire-level, machine-checkable definition of a delegation edge, including how it is allowed to fail.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The classic task-allocation protocol: announce, bid, award. Modern delegation edges rediscover it repeatedly.</sub> | **Industry standard**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[BDI Agents: From Theory to Practice](https://cdn.aaai.org/ICMAS/1995/ICMAS95-042.pdf)**<br><sub>Paper · Agent architectures</sub> | **ICMAS 1995**<br><sub>Anand S. Rao, Michael P. Georgeff · 1995</sub> | Connects belief-desire-intention logic to a working interpreter, showing how an agent commits to intentions, when it drops or reconsiders them, and how commitment strategies trade responsiveness against thrashing. The reference statement of what a single bounded agent's decision loop consists of, drawn from PRS and dMARS deployments.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The belief-desire-intention model that gave agents an internal structure worth reasoning about rather than a prompt.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Gaia Methodology for Agent-Oriented Analysis and Design](https://doi.org/10.1023/A:1010071910869)**<br><sub>Paper · Agent-oriented software engineering</sub> | **Autonomous Agents and Multi-Agent Systems 3, 285-312**<br><sub>Michael Wooldridge, Nicholas R. Jennings, David Kinny · 2000</sub> | Treats a multi-agent system as an organization to be designed, with an analysis phase producing roles (responsibilities, permissions, activities, protocols) and a design phase producing an agent model, services model, and acquaintance model. The acquaintance model is literally the communication graph, made an explicit deliverable rather than an emergent property.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> An early methodology treating agent systems as organizations with roles and interactions, decades before the current label.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Developing Organised Multi-Agent Systems Using the MOISE+ Model](https://doi.org/10.1504/IJAOSE.2007.016266)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization models for multi-agent systems</sub> | **International Journal of Agent-Oriented Software Engineering 1, 370-395**<br><sub>Jomi F. Hübner, Jaime S. Sichman, Olivier Boissier · 2007</sub> | Specifies an agent organization as a first-class, machine-readable artifact in three separable parts: structural (roles, groups, inheritance, intra- and inter-group links), functional (global goals decomposed into missions and schemes), and deontic (which role is permitted or obliged to take which mission). The closest pre-LLM precedent for a declared, inspectable org graph decoupled from the agents that fill it.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Separates the organization specification from the agents filling it, which is exactly the org-graph-versus-instance distinction.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Trends in Cooperative Distributed Problem Solving](https://doi.org/10.1109/69.43404)**<br><sub>Paper · Blackboard and distributed problem solving</sub> | **IEEE Transactions on Knowledge and Data Engineering 1, 63-83**<br><sub>Edmund H. Durfee, Victor R. Lesser, Daniel D. Corkill · 1989</sub> | Surveys how nodes that each hold partial, inconsistent information can still solve a problem jointly, covering task decomposition, result synthesis, organizational structuring, and partial global planning. Names the failure modes (distraction, redundant work, conflicting local views) that reappear whenever agents are given overlapping scope.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Surveys how distributed problem solvers divide work and share results, the questions topology research still asks.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Interdisciplinary Study of Coordination](https://doi.org/10.1145/174666.174668)**<br><sub>Paper · Coordination theory</sub> | **ACM Computing Surveys 26, 87-119**<br><sub>Thomas W. Malone, Kevin Crowston · 1994</sub> | Defines coordination as managing dependencies between activities, then enumerates dependency types (shared resource, producer/consumer, simultaneity, task/subtask) and the coordination mechanisms available for each. Gives a vocabulary for saying precisely what an edge in an org or run graph is carrying, drawn from computer science, economics, and organization theory at once.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Defines coordination as managing dependencies between activities, a definition that makes edges the unit of design.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[End-to-End Arguments in System Design](https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2, 277-288**<br><sub>J. H. Saltzer, D. P. Reed, D. D. Clark · 1984</sub> | Argues that a function can usually only be implemented correctly at the endpoints that know the application semantics, so lower layers should not try to guarantee it; low-level versions are justified only as performance optimizations. Directly bears on where verification and correctness checks belong in an agent pipeline: the orchestrator cannot check what only the endpoint understands.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The argument for placing correctness checks at the endpoints, which decides whether a gate belongs at the edge or in the middle.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[A Note on Distributed Computing](https://scholar.harvard.edu/files/waldo/files/waldo-94.pdf)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **Sun Microsystems Laboratories Technical Report TR-94-29**<br><sub>Jim Waldo, Geoff Wyant, Ann Wollrath, Sam Kendall · 1994</sub> | Shows that hiding the local/remote distinction behind a uniform call interface fails, because remote interaction differs irreducibly in latency, memory access, concurrency, and above all partial failure. The standing argument against pretending a delegation to another agent is just a function call.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Explains why pretending remote calls are local fails, the mistake every agent handoff abstraction is tempted to make.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Byzantine Generals Problem](https://doi.org/10.1145/357172.357176)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems 4, 382-401**<br><sub>Leslie Lamport, Robert Shostak, Marshall Pease · 1982</sub> | Proves that with unsigned messages, agreement among n participants is impossible unless more than two-thirds are loyal, and that signatures change the bound. The formal ceiling on what voting, cross-checking, or majority-of-agents schemes can deliver when some participants produce arbitrarily wrong output.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The canonical result on agreeing in the presence of faulty participants, the theory behind quorum gates.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System](https://doi.org/10.1145/359545.359563)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **Communications of the ACM 21, 558-565**<br><sub>Leslie Lamport · 1978</sub> | Defines the happened-before partial order and logical clocks, showing that causality, not wall-clock time, is what a distributed system can actually observe. The formal basis for run-graph lineage: which agent's output could possibly have influenced which other, when there is no global clock.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Ordering events without a global clock is the problem shared state across parallel agents actually has.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 📄 **[Sagas](https://doi.org/10.1145/38713.38742)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **ACM SIGMOD 1987**<br><sub>Hector Garcia-Molina, Kenneth Salem · 1987</sub> | Introduces the saga: a long-running unit of work split into a sequence of committed sub-transactions, each with a compensating action that semantically undoes it if a later step fails. The pattern behind rollback and repair in long agent workflows where holding a lock across the whole run is not an option.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The compensating-transaction pattern that gives a long-running graph a defined way to undo partial work.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📄 **[Life beyond Distributed Transactions: an Apostate's Opinion](https://www.cidrdb.org/cidr2007/papers/cidr07p15.pdf)**<br><sub>Paper · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **CIDR 2007; revised in ACM Queue 14, 2016**<br><sub>Pat Helland · 2007</sub> | Argues that at scale you give up distributed transactions and instead structure the world as entities (atomically updatable alone) plus activities that coordinate them through messages, which forces every message to be made idempotent and every partner relationship to carry state. A blueprint for scoping agent state and making retries safe.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Argues for designing around the absence of distributed transactions, which is the situation agent state is already in.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 📝 **[Fallacies of Distributed Computing Explained](https://arnon.me/wp-content/uploads/Files/fallacies.pdf)**<br><sub>Blog · Distributed systems principles</sub> | **Self-published whitepaper**<br><sub>Arnon Rotem-Gal-Oz · 2006</sub> | Works through the eight fallacies attributed to L. Peter Deutsch and James Gosling at Sun (the network is reliable, latency is zero, bandwidth is infinite, the network is secure, topology doesn't change, there is one administrator, transport cost is zero, the network is homogeneous) with the consequences of each. The compact checklist of assumptions that quietly break once agents run on separate machines under separate owners.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The assumptions that break distributed systems, restated clearly, and every one of them applies to an agent fleet.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Nature of the Firm](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1468-0335.1937.tb00002.x)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization theory</sub> | **Economica 4, 386-405**<br><sub>Ronald H. Coase · 1937</sub> | Asks why firms exist rather than everything being contracted on the open market, and answers with transaction costs: a firm grows until the cost of organizing one more task internally equals the cost of buying it outside. The framework for deciding when to add a standing agent role versus spawning a one-off task.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Transaction costs decide what belongs inside one organization, which is the economics behind adding or cutting a node.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Organization Design: An Information Processing View](https://doi.org/10.1287/inte.4.3.28)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization theory</sub> | **Interfaces 4, 28-36**<br><sub>Jay R. Galbraith · 1974</sub> | Treats task uncertainty as the driver of structure: the more uncertain the work, the more information must be processed during execution, and organizations respond either by reducing the need (slack resources, self-contained units) or by increasing capacity (vertical information systems, lateral links). A direct design lever for choosing between hierarchical routing and peer-to-peer agent links.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Treats organization structure as a response to information-processing load, a direct analogue for topology under uncertainty.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📕 **[The Structuring of Organizations: A Synthesis of the Research](https://mintzberg.org/books/structuring-organizations-synthesis-research)**<br><sub>Book · Organization theory</sub> | **mintzberg.org**<br><sub>Henry Mintzberg · 1979</sub> | Reduces all organizational coordination to five mechanisms (mutual adjustment, direct supervision, standardization of work processes, of outputs, and of skills) and builds five structural configurations from them. Gives a finite, checkable taxonomy for what an edge between two agents actually enforces: a supervisor, a shared spec, or an informal channel.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A structural vocabulary for organizations that maps onto agent topologies more precisely than ad hoc diagrams.</sub> | **Book/course**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Exploration and Exploitation in Organizational Learning](https://doi.org/10.1287/orsc.2.1.71)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization theory</sub> | **Organization Science 2, 71-87**<br><sub>James G. March · 1991</sub> | Models the tension between exploring new possibilities and exploiting known ones, showing with a simulation that fast learners converging on shared code can lower long-run collective performance because they stop generating variety. The reason a fleet of agents that all adopt the same successful strategy gets worse, not better.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The exploration-exploitation tension in organizations, which is what a self-modifying graph is really trading off.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |
| 📕 **[The Mythical Man-Month: Essays on Software Engineering, Anniversary Edition](https://www.informit.com/store/mythical-man-month-essays-on-software-engineering-anniversary-9780201835953)**<br><sub>Book · Organization theory</sub> | **Addison-Wesley Professional**<br><sub>Frederick P. Brooks Jr. · 1995</sub> | Brooks's law (adding people to a late project makes it later) rests on communication paths growing as n(n-1)/2, and the surgical team chapter proposes an asymmetric structure where one person does the work and the rest are specialized support. The 1995 edition adds a twenty-year retrospective and reprints No Silver Bullet.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Communication overhead growing faster than headcount is why adding agent nodes stops helping.</sub> | **Book/course**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[How Do Committees Invent?](https://www.melconway.com/Home/Committees_Paper.html)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization theory</sub> | **Datamation, April 1968**<br><sub>Melvin E. Conway · 1968</sub> | The original article behind Conway's law, whose thesis appears three paragraphs from the end: organizations that design systems are constrained to produce designs that copy their own communication structures. Full text on the author's site, with his later note on why the argument is laid out that way.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The original statement of the law linking communication structure to system design, applicable to agents as literally as to teams.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Architecture of Complexity](https://www.jstor.org/stable/985254)**<br><sub>Paper · Organization theory</sub> | **Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society 106, 467-482**<br><sub>Herbert A. Simon · 1962</sub> | Argues that complex systems that survive are hierarchic and nearly decomposable: interactions within a subsystem are strong and frequent, interactions across subsystems are weak and slow, which is why such systems can be assembled and understood at all. The theoretical case for cutting an agent organization at low-traffic boundaries.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Why complex systems are hierarchical and nearly decomposable, an argument for the shape most agent organizations converge on.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📕 **[The Society of Mind](https://www.simonandschuster.com/books/Society-Of-Mind/Marvin-Minsky/9780671657130)**<br><sub>Book · Agent architectures</sub> | **Simon &amp; Schuster**<br><sub>Marvin Minsky · 1986</sub> | Builds intelligence out of large numbers of small, individually mindless agents organized into agencies, with chapters on how they are recruited, how conflicts between them are settled, and how censors and suppressors block bad plans. Written as 270 one-page essays; the source of the intuition that capability is an emergent property of an organization of narrow parts.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Intelligence from many simple interacting agents, the intellectual precedent for treating a mind as an organization.</sub> | **Book/course**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[A Brief History of Stigmergy](https://doi.org/10.1162/106454699568700)**<br><sub>Paper · Coordination theory</sub> | **Artificial Life 5, 97-116**<br><sub>Guy Theraulaz, Eric Bonabeau · 1999</sub> | Traces Grassé's 1959 concept of stigmergy, where insects coordinate not by talking to each other but by modifying a shared environment that then triggers the next action, and distinguishes quantitative from qualitative variants. The formal account of indirect coordination through shared artifacts, with no messages and no coordinator.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Coordination through traces left in a shared environment, the mechanism behind agents that coordinate via artifacts.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Evolution</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Application of Petri Nets to Workflow Management](https://doi.org/10.1142/S0218126698000043)**<br><sub>Paper · Workflow and dataflow theory</sub> | **Journal of Circuits, Systems and Computers 8, 21-66**<br><sub>Wil M. P. van der Aalst · 1998</sub> | Defines WF-nets, a restricted class of Petri nets for workflows, and gives a decidable soundness criterion: every case can still reach completion, completion leaves no tokens behind, and no transition is dead. Shows what it means to statically verify that a task graph cannot deadlock or strand work before you run it.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Formal workflow semantics developed long before agent DAGs, including the soundness questions those DAGs ignore.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |
| 📕 **[Multiagent Systems: Algorithmic, Game-Theoretic, and Logical Foundations](https://www.masfoundations.org/)**<br><sub>Book · Foundational texts</sub> | **Cambridge University Press**<br><sub>Yoav Shoham, Kevin Leyton-Brown · 2009</sub> | The standard graduate reference on multiagent systems — distributed constraint satisfaction, coalition formation, mechanism design, social choice — with a free uncorrected PDF distributed by the authors under their Cambridge contract. The formal grounding for delegation, verification, and escalation edges predating LLM agents.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The standard textbook for the algorithmic and game-theoretic side of multi-agent systems.</sub> | **Book/course**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📕 **[An Introduction to MultiAgent Systems (2nd edition)](https://www.cs.ox.ac.uk/people/michael.wooldridge/pubs/imas/)**<br><sub>Book · Foundational texts</sub> | **John Wiley &amp; Sons**<br><sub>Michael Wooldridge · 2009</sub> | The standard undergraduate textbook for classical multi-agent systems; the 2009 second edition adds chapters on ontologies, voting, auctions, bargaining, coalition formation, and argumentation. Author's page carries errata and slide decks.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The standard introductory text on multi-agent systems, and the fastest way to stop reinventing its vocabulary.</sub> | **Book/course**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Topological Structure Learning Should Be A Research Priority for LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.22467)**<br><sub>Paper · Topology research agenda</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Jiaxi Yang, Mengqi Zhang, Yiqiao Jin, Hao Chen, Qingsong Wen, Lu Lin, Yi He, Srijan Kumar, Weijie Xu, James Evans, Jindong Wang · 2025</sub> | A position paper arguing that how agents are connected is neglected relative to how they are prompted, and proposing a three-stage agenda of agent selection, structure profiling, and topology synthesis, tied to adaptability, efficiency, robustness, and fairness. Revised October 2025.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> States the case that topology is a first-class design variable, and names the stages a team would work through to choose one deliberately.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

| 📚 **[Claude Code: Orchestrate subagents at scale with dynamic workflows](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/workflows)**<br><sub>Docs · Script-driven orchestration</sub> | **code.claude.com**<br><sub>Anthropic · 2026</sub> | Official docs for the clearest shipping example of deterministic-code orchestration: Claude writes a JavaScript workflow script that holds the plan, fans out up to 1,000 subagents (16 concurrent) via agent() and pipeline(), keeps intermediate results in script variables instead of any context window, and supports resume with cached agent results. The comparison table of who-holds-the-plan across subagents, skills, agent teams, and workflows is a compact statement of the model-driven vs script-driven orchestration tradeoff.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The clearest shipping example of code orchestrating agents deterministically, with fan-out and verification expressed as ordinary control flow.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

It is the most-forked starting point for anyone building their own agent town, and its replay artifact is an early example of treating a run as an inspectable record rather than a transcript. The repository has not been updated since August 2024 and is a frozen research artifact rather than an actively developed project.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A runnable society plus a replay viewer, which makes a multi-agent run an inspectable record rather than a wall of transcript.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |

Defaults to local inference via Ollama, making agent-society experiments cheap to run without API spend.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A cheap local-inference starting point for society experiments, built on a backend that gives shared state real transactions.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 🧰 **[google-deepmind/concordia](https://github.com/google-deepmind/concordia)**<br><sub>Tool · Simulation platforms</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Google DeepMind · 2023</sub> | Apache-licensed library for generative agent-based models, structured around Entities, composable Components (memory, chain-of-thought, logic), and an Engine that runs the loop. Component-level composition means an agent's reasoning structure is declared as data you can diff and re-run, not hand-written prose.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Component-level composition means an agent's reasoning structure is declared as data you can diff and re-run.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

Spawned mcp-scan, the OWASP MCP Top 10 tool-poisoning category, and the MCPTox benchmark.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Tool descriptions are an injection surface, which means an edge can be compromised by the thing it connects to.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |

Finds all agent-to-agent protocols use hybrid payloads with persistent sessions, and predicts a federated layered protocol stack rather than one winner — the current best map of where edge protocols are converging.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Compares nine protocols along dimensions that matter in implementation rather than in marketing.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[Beyond Message Passing: A Semantic View of Agent Communication Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02369)**<br><sub>Paper · Surveys &amp; taxonomies</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Dun Yuan, Fuyuan Lyu, et al. (McGill/MILA) · 2026</sub> | Evaluates 18 protocols across communication, syntactic, and semantic layers and lands the sharpest critique in the space: transport and schema support are maturing, but clarification, context alignment, and verification have no protocol-level support — so semantic coordination gets pushed into prompts. Directly frames why handoff quality, not wire format, is the open problem.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The sharpest critique of current protocols: they standardize transport while leaving meaning to convention.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📐 **[AG-UI: the Agent-User Interaction Protocol](https://github.com/ag-ui-protocol/ag-ui)**<br><sub>Standard · UI protocol layer</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>CopilotKit team and framework partners · 2025</sub> | Open event-based protocol for the agent-to-user edge: the frontend POSTs once, then consumes a typed SSE event stream (messages, tool calls, state patches, human-in-the-loop interrupts) from any agent backend. Completes the protocol triad — MCP for tools, A2A for agents, AG-UI for users — and has first-party integrations in Microsoft Agent Framework, AG2, and LangGraph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The agent-to-user edge deserves a contract too, and this is the protocol that gives it typed events.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |

Cites the MAST finding that most multi-agent failures are coordination, not capability.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Compares shared, isolated, and hybrid memory directly, which is the decision every multi-agent design must make explicitly.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>State</sub> |

The token-economics framing for shared-vs-isolated state decisions.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Treats the context window as a budgeted resource, which is the discipline that keeps long-running nodes coherent.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Experimental (flag-gated) as of v2.1.x, July 2026.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A shipping implementation of shared task state with ownership rules, which is the concrete form of coordination infrastructure.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>State</sub> |

A clean example of hierarchical (global/team/private) memory scoping.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> One scoped memory API across a team, showing how a framework resolves whose memory is whose.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Framework-agnostic — the same memory layer serves agents built in LangGraph, CrewAI, AutoGen/AG2, and Strands.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Scoping every write by user, agent, and run is the practical pattern that keeps shared memory from becoming noise.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Notably honest about concurrency: memory_insert is append-only and safe under simultaneous writers, while rethink-style rewrites can clobber each other.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Memory blocks attached to many agents make shared state explicit and immediately visible to all readers.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Python, TypeScript, and Rust clients; integrates with Claude Code as a memory backend.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Self-hostable memory infrastructure for teams that need shared recall without sending state to a vendor.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |

The main off-the-shelf option for graph-shaped shared memory serving an agent organization; ships an MCP server so any agent can attach.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Temporal shared state across many agents, where facts carry validity intervals rather than being overwritten.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Beats MemGPT on Deep Memory Retrieval (94.8% vs 93.4%).<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Bi-temporal memory answers when a fact was true and when it was learned, which matters when nodes disagree.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>State</sub> |

From the author of the widely used RAG_Techniques and GenAI_Agents repos; the fastest hands-on path through this section's ideas.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Runnable notebooks for memory patterns, including a shared multi-agent case, which shortens the path from idea to test.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 🧰 **[Firecracker](https://github.com/firecracker-microvm/firecracker)**<br><sub>Tool · Worker isolation</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Amazon Web Services · 2026</sub> | Apache-2.0 virtual machine monitor built on Linux KVM that runs workloads in minimal microVMs, giving each one its own kernel while keeping the startup cost and density closer to containers than to full virtual machines. Around 35.9k stars and the isolation layer beneath several commercial agent sandboxes.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Hardware-level isolation per worker is what makes it safe to run many agents writing untrusted code side by side.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 🧰 **[E2B](https://github.com/e2b-dev/E2B)**<br><sub>Tool · Worker isolation</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>E2B · 2026</sub> | Apache-2.0 infrastructure for running AI-generated code in isolated cloud sandboxes, with Python and JavaScript SDKs for creating sandboxes, executing commands, and managing filesystem state, plus Terraform-based self-hosting on AWS, GCP, and Azure. Around 13.2k stars and actively developed.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Gives each node a disposable filesystem and shell, which is the practical unit of isolation when workers run in parallel.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 🧰 **[gVisor](https://github.com/google/gvisor)**<br><sub>Tool · Worker isolation</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Google · 2026</sub> | Apache-2.0 OCI runtime implementing a Linux-compatible kernel in userspace Go, so a sandboxed process's syscalls are serviced by gVisor rather than reaching the host kernel directly. Around 19k stars, and the alternative isolation model to microVMs, trading some compatibility for faster startup.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The other mainstream isolation choice for agent workers, and the tradeoff against microVMs is a real design decision.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>State</sub> |

Faustino; Guanheng Liu; Shan Zhang; Hongbin Luo; Suhaib A. Fahmy; Zafar A. Qazi; Marco Canini · 2026</sub> | Standardizes configuration and execution for 12 representative multi-agent systems and exports framework-agnostic traces with latency, cost, reliability, and failure signals.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Enables controlled comparison across architectures and repeated runs while revealing that topology can dominate model or tool changes in resource and reliability profiles.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[A2A Protocol Technology Compatibility Kit](https://github.com/a2aproject/a2a-tck)**<br><sub>Tool · Protocol conformance</sub> | **Agent2Agent Project, Linux Foundation**<br><sub>Agent2Agent Project · 2026</sub> | Tests Agent2Agent protocol implementations across gRPC, JSON-RPC, and HTTP with JSON and emits machine-readable, HTML, and JUnit conformance reports.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Turns interoperability claims at agent handoff edges into executable gates that can run in development and continuous integration.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Debating with More Persuasive LLMs Leads to More Truthful Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.06782)**<br><sub>Paper · Adversarial verification / debate</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Akbir Khan, John Hughes, Dan Valentine, et al. · 2024</sub> | ICML 2024 best-paper showing that a weak judge overseeing two strong models arguing opposing sides gets more truthful answers — and that optimizing debaters for persuasiveness improves judge accuracy. The strongest empirical case for adversarial (rather than cooperative) verifier edges in scalable-oversight settings.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A weak judge overseeing strong debaters reaching better answers is the theoretical basis for using cheaper verifiers.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Peacemaker or Troublemaker: How Sycophancy Shapes Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23055)**<br><sub>Paper · Anti-groupthink</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Binwei Yao, Chao Shang, Wanyu Du, et al. (AWS AI) · 2025</sub> | Identifies sycophancy-driven "disagreement collapse" — panels converging on harmony before reaching the correct answer — in both peer debate and centralized-judge topologies, and proposes design principles to keep disagreement productive. Core reading on why councils fail as verification gates.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Names disagreement collapse, where panels converge on harmony before reaching correctness, and the conditions that cause it.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[The Cost of Consensus: Isolated Self-Correction Prevails Over Unguided Homogeneous Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914)**<br><sub>Paper · Debate skepticism</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Blaž Bertalanič, Carolina Fortuna · 2026</sub> | April 2026 result showing independent self-correction by isolated models beats teams of identical models debating without structure, at a fraction of the compute. Reinforces the design rule that debate edges only pay off with heterogeneous agents and structured protocols.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Finds isolated self-correction beating identical-model teams, which sets a hard baseline for any panel design.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[Reflection Agents](https://www.langchain.com/blog/reflection-agents)**<br><sub>Blog · Evaluator loops as topology</sub> | **langchain.com**<br><sub>Ankush Gola (LangChain) · 2024</sub> | Implements three evaluator-loop topologies in LangGraph — basic generator/reflector cycles, Reflexion with evidence-grounded critique, and LATS combining reflection with Monte Carlo tree search. The practical bridge from reflection-as-prompting to reflection-as-graph-structure, with runnable code.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Implements generator and reflector cycles concretely, which is the smallest useful verification structure in a graph.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Proof-or-Stop: Don't Trust the Agent, Trust the Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14890)**<br><sub>Paper · Evidence-gated lifecycle control</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Jek Huang, Jeffery Hsia, Jiayi Sun, Freddie Shi, Wei Huang, Ian H. White · 2026</sub> | Treats every agent claim ("tests passed", "reviewed") as an unverified assertion and only allows lifecycle transitions backed by fresh, traceable, mechanically checkable evidence read by gates. Reports zero false-positive completion claims across 10 scenarios, rejection of 18 evidence-tampering methods, and hidden-failure amplification cut from 1.7% to 0.1%; published July 16, 2026, days before the graph-engineering discourse took off.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Treats every claim as unverified until evidence is produced, the strictest available formulation of an evidence gate.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧪 **[JudgeBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating LLM-based Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.12784)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Judge evaluation</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Sijun Tan, Siyuan Zhuang, Kyle Montgomery, et al. · 2024</sub> | Benchmarks judges on response pairs where factual correctness — not style — determines the right verdict, exposing how badly judges do on hard knowledge, reasoning, math, and coding comparisons. The go-to number for deciding whether a candidate judge model is strong enough to sit on a gating edge. Published at ICLR 2025.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Benchmarks judges on cases where correctness rather than style decides, exposing judges that reward polish.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Justice or Prejudice? Quantifying Biases in LLM-as-a-Judge (CALM)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02736)**<br><sub>Paper · Judge failure modes</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Jiayi Ye, Yanbo Wang, Yue Huang, et al. · 2024</sub> | The CALM framework automatically injects perturbations to quantify 12 distinct bias types in LLM judges, giving a systematic audit checklist for any judge node before it is trusted as a gate. Complements the anecdotal bias lists in earlier work with automated measurement.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Quantifies twelve distinct judge biases, which turns judge selection into an engineering decision with known failure modes.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Large Language Models are not Fair Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.17926)**<br><sub>Paper · Judge failure modes</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Peiyi Wang, Lei Li, Liang Chen, et al. · 2023</sub> | Shows that judge rankings can be flipped simply by reordering candidate answers in the prompt, letting weaker models "beat" stronger ones. Proposes the standard mitigations — position swapping, multiple evidence generation, human-in-the-loop calibration — that any pairwise judge node should implement.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Reordering candidates can flip a judge's ranking, which means a gate's prompt structure is part of its correctness.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Self-Preference Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.21819)**<br><sub>Paper · Judge failure modes</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>arXiv · 2024</sub> | Quantifies GPT-4's significant preference for its own outputs and traces the mechanism to perplexity: judges favor text that is familiar to them. Concrete design implication for graphs: never let the same model family both generate and gate its own work without cross-model checks.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Judges prefer their own outputs, a decisive argument against evaluating a node with the same model that produced the work.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧰 **[DeepEval](https://github.com/confident-ai/deepeval)**<br><sub>Tool · Judge infrastructure</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Confident AI · 2026</sub> | Pytest-style open-source LLM evaluation framework (16.9k stars, v4.1.0 July 2026) with G-Eval judge metrics, a DAG builder for deterministic decision-tree evaluation, and agentic metrics (task completion, tool correctness, plan adherence). One of the few tools that treats deterministic gates and model judges as composable alternatives in the same graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Test-style evaluation makes gates part of the development loop rather than a separate review exercise.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧰 **[Verdict: a library for compound LLM-judge systems](https://github.com/haizelabs/verdict)**<br><sub>Tool · Judge infrastructure</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Nimit Kalra, Leonard Tang (Haize Labs) · 2025</sub> | Open-source framework that composes judges from graph primitives (Units, Layers, Blocks) into hierarchical verification, debate, and aggregation pipelines over arbitrary dependency graphs — literally judges as subgraphs. Claims SOTA-competitive evaluation accuracy without fine-tuning by scaling judge-time compute; MIT-licensed.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Composing judges from graph primitives makes a verification stage a designed structure rather than one prompt.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[Evaluating the Effectiveness of LLM-Evaluators (aka LLM-as-Judge)](https://eugeneyan.com/writing/llm-evaluators/)**<br><sub>Blog · Judge reliability in practice</sub> | **eugeneyan.com**<br><sub>Eugene Yan · 2024</sub> | Literature-grounded practitioner review of when LLM evaluators work: pairwise comparison is more stable than direct scoring, classification metrics suit objective checks while correlations suit subjective ones, and recall on factual-inconsistency detection can be alarmingly low (30-60%) even with high precision. A calibration reference before trusting a judge as a gate.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A literature-grounded account of when model judges work, including why pairwise comparison beats absolute scoring.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Who Validates the Validators? Aligning LLM-Assisted Evaluation with Human Preferences (EvalGen)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.12272)**<br><sub>Paper · Judge validation</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Shreya Shankar, J.D. Zamfirescu-Pereira, Björn Hartmann, Aditya Parameswaran, Ian Arawjo · 2024</sub> | Introduces EvalGen and documents "criteria drift": humans only discover their real evaluation criteria by grading outputs, so judge rubrics can never be fully specified upfront. Explains why gate criteria in agent graphs must be iteratively co-developed with humans, not written once.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Documents criteria drift, where people only discover their real standards by looking at outputs, which reshapes how rubrics get written.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[A Survey on LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.15594)**<br><sub>Paper · LLM-as-judge foundations</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Jiawei Gu, Xuhui Jiang, Zhichao Shi, et al. · 2024</sub> | Comprehensive survey of how to build reliable judge systems: consistency strategies, bias mitigation, and adaptation across evaluation scenarios. Useful as the single starting map before choosing a judge-node design for a given edge in the graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The comprehensive survey of building reliable judges, covering consistency strategies and bias mitigation together.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05685)**<br><sub>Paper · LLM-as-judge foundations</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Lianmin Zheng, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, et al. (LMSYS) · 2023</sub> | The paper that legitimized model judges as system components: GPT-4 judges reach over 80% agreement with human preferences, matching human-human agreement. Also the canonical catalog of judge failure modes — position bias, verbosity bias, self-enhancement bias, and limited reasoning — that every judge node in an agent graph must be designed around.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The result that legitimized model judges as system components, with measured agreement against human preference.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.11171)**<br><sub>Paper · Majority vote as topology</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Xuezhi Wang, Jason Wei, Denny Zhou, et al. (Google) · 2022</sub> | The original sample-many-paths-and-majority-vote result (ICLR 2023): agreement across diverse reasoning paths is a cheap correctness signal. In graph terms, the simplest fan-out/fan-in verification topology, and the baseline every fancier debate or judge structure must beat.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Majority voting over diverse reasoning paths is the cheapest quorum available, and the baseline elaborate panels must beat.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Let's Verify Step by Step](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.20050)**<br><sub>Paper · Process reward models</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Hunter Lightman, Vineet Kosaraju, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, Karl Cobbe (OpenAI) · 2023</sub> | Process supervision (rewarding each reasoning step) substantially outperforms outcome supervision for reliability, with the PRM800K dataset of 800K step-level labels. The foundation for using process reward models as fine-grained gates on intermediate agent steps rather than only on final outputs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Rewarding each step rather than only the outcome is what makes verification usable mid-run instead of after it.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Minority Sentinel: When to Overturn Majority Voting in Multi-Agent LLM Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29270)**<br><sub>Paper · Quorum voting</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Chuan He, Zebin Chen, Zhengyi Yang, et al. · 2026</sub> | Trains a meta-classifier that detects when a debate majority is suppressing a correct minority opinion and decides when to overturn the vote, hitting 81.2% override accuracy across six benchmarks. Turns quorum voting from a fixed aggregation rule into an adaptive gate — directly applicable to voting nodes in agent graphs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Detecting when a majority is suppressing a correct minority addresses the central weakness of quorum gates.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[Creating a LLM-as-a-Judge That Drives Business Results (Critique Shadowing)](https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/llm-judge/)**<br><sub>Blog · Rubric design for judges</sub> | **hamel.dev**<br><sub>Hamel Husain · 2024</sub> | The most-cited practitioner methodology for building trustworthy judges: anchor on one principal domain expert, force binary pass/fail verdicts instead of Likert scores, and bootstrap the judge prompt from expert critiques ("critique shadowing"). Directly transferable to designing gate nodes whose verdicts humans will actually trust.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The most-cited practitioner methodology for judges people actually trust, anchored on a single domain expert.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[G-Eval: NLG Evaluation using GPT-4 with Better Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16634)**<br><sub>Paper · Rubric design for judges</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Yang Liu, Dan Iter, Yichong Xu, Shuohang Wang, Ruochen Xu, Chenguang Zhu · 2023</sub> | Introduced the chain-of-thought, form-filling rubric pattern that most production judge prompts still follow, and was among the first to flag that LLM evaluators are biased toward LLM-generated text. Directly relevant to writing rubrics for judge nodes rather than asking for bare scores.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The chain-of-thought rubric pattern most production judge prompts still follow, worth reading in the original.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Large Language Monkeys: Scaling Inference Compute with Repeated Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21787)**<br><sub>Paper · Verification gap</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Bradley Brown, Jordan Juravsky, et al. (Stanford) · 2024</sub> | Coverage grows log-linearly with samples (SWE-bench Lite: 15.9% to 56% at 250 samples) in domains with automatic verifiers, but majority voting and reward models plateau after a few hundred samples where no oracle exists. The cleanest quantification of why the verifier — not the generator — is the binding constraint on graph designs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Repeated sampling raises coverage sharply where a verifier exists, which is precisely why gates unlock parallelism.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[The AI Co-Scientist Is Here. The Bottleneck Is Verification.](https://www.k-dense.ai/blog/ai-co-scientist-verification-bottleneck)**<br><sub>Blog · Verification-bottleneck essays</sub> | **k-dense.ai**<br><sub>K-Dense · 2026</sub> | June 2026 essay on agentic science arguing "discovery is becoming cheap, trust is not," and specifying a concrete verification stack for research agents: source grounding, executable inspectable code, data provenance, preserved intermediate artifacts, and human approval checkpoints. Coins "research laundering" for unverifiable agent output.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Argues discovery is becoming cheap while trust is not, and specifies what verification would have to provide.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[Verification Is the New Bottleneck — Not Generation](https://danielkeller.com/tech/verification-not-generation/)**<br><sub>Blog · Verification-bottleneck essays</sub> | **danielkeller.com**<br><sub>Daniel Keller · 2026</sub> | July 3, 2026 essay proposing a Swiss-cheese verification stack for agent pipelines: deterministic checks (static analysis, tests, security scans) first, LLM-as-judge for semantic correctness second, and humans reviewing evidence rather than artifacts last. Recommends spending 30% of effort on generation and 70% on verification infrastructure.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Proposes a layered verification stack for agent pipelines, combining deterministic checks with model judgment.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📝 **[Verification Is the Next Bottleneck in AI-Assisted Development](https://web.archive.org/web/20260611071957/https://www.opslane.com/blog/verification-bottleneck)**<br><sub>Blog · Verification-bottleneck essays</sub> | **opslane.com via Internet Archive**<br><sub>Abhishek Ray · 2026</sub> | April 2026 essay arguing that automation cost is collapsing while "the cost to verify is biologically bounded": teams shipping 40-50 agent PRs a week drown reviewers. Prescribes a three-layer gate stack — test suites, upstream human acceptance criteria, and adversarial "agents verifying agents" — with humans reserved for critical changes. The original post was removed from opslane.com and now returns 404; this is the Internet Archive capture from June 2026.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Frames verification cost as the binding constraint on automation, which is the economic case for gate design.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Generative Verifiers: Reward Modeling as Next-Token Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.15240)**<br><sub>Paper · Verifier models as gates</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Lunjun Zhang, Arian Hosseini, et al. (Google DeepMind) · 2024</sub> | Trains verifiers as generative next-token predictors (GenRM) that can reason before scoring and exploit test-time compute, outperforming discriminative reward models. Blurs the line between judge nodes and reward-model gates — the same node can deliberate and then gate. Published at ICLR 2025.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Verifiers that reason before scoring and can explain themselves are more useful as graph nodes than opaque scorers.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[TraceFix: Repairing Agent Coordination Protocols with TLA+ Counterexamples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07935)**<br><sub>Paper · Protocol verification</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Shuren Xia, Qiwei Li, Taqiya Ehsan, Jorge Ortiz · 2026</sub> | Model-checks agent coordination protocols in TLA+ and repairs them from counterexamples until verification passes: all 48 tasks across 16 scenario families verified, 62.5% on the first attempt and none needing more than four repair cycles. Across 3,456 runs, verified protocols cut deadlock and livelock from 31.1% to 14.1%.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Shows a coordination protocol can be formally checked and repaired before deployment, rather than debugged from production incidents.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |

A case study in confabulation cascades — the agent's false model of the world compounding into destructive action — that made agent kill-switches a mainstream requirement.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The clearest cautionary record of agents destroying production data, and why irreversible actions need gates and human control paths.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |

Positions independent validation between nodes as the most underused reliability mechanism in multi-agent systems.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Maps observed production symptoms to coordination causes, which is the translation layer between an incident and a design fix.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |

Answers the partial-failure question — agent 3 of 5 died after sending real emails — that plain retry logic cannot.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Compensating transactions give a graph a defined way to undo partial work, the pattern distributed systems settled on decades ago.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |

The strongest example of eval-in-production as the control loop for a deployed agent graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Online scoring catches regressions in a running organization rather than in a pre-launch evaluation only.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
