The lightweight entry point for seeing what an agent fleet actually spends.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> One proxy line gives a graph request logging, caching, and cost attribution without touching node code.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[LiteLLM](https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm)**<br><sub>Tool · Gateways and cost control</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>BerriAI · 2026</sub> | The de facto open-source AI gateway (~54k stars, used by Netflix and Stripe): 100+ providers behind one interface, with multi-tenant cost tracking, virtual keys, and per-key/per-team/per-project budget enforcement at 8ms P95 overhead. In graph terms, this is where budget allocation across nodes becomes an enforced policy rather than a spreadsheet — give each agent role its own key and spending cap.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A single interface across providers is what makes per-node model swaps a configuration change rather than a rewrite.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[Portkey](https://portkey.ai/)**<br><sub>Tool · Gateways and cost control</sub> | **portkey.ai**<br><sub>Portkey · 2026</sub> | AI gateway and governance layer (acquired by Palo Alto Networks; 3,000+ teams, 3T+ tokens/day claimed) with per-team and per-use-case budget limits, real-time cost and anomaly dashboards, RBAC, guardrails with PII redaction, and 1,600+ models behind one API. Representative of the governance end of paying for the graph: budgets, audit, and access control per organizational unit.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Governance at the gateway lets budget and routing policy apply to every node without trusting each one to behave.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[FrugalGPT: How to Use Large Language Models While Reducing Cost and Improving Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.05176)**<br><sub>Paper · Model-tier assignment</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Lingjiao Chen, Matei Zaharia, James Zou (Stanford) · 2023</sub> | The original LLM-cascade economics paper: routing queries through cheap-to-expensive model chains matches GPT-4 quality with up to 98% cost reduction, or beats it by 4% at equal cost. Predates the agent-graph framing but is the intellectual ancestor of every cheap-worker/frontier-escalation tier design.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The original cascade-economics result: routing cheap-to-expensive matches strong-model quality at a fraction of the cost.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[Optimizing Model Selection for Compound AI Systems (LLMSelector)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14815)**<br><sub>Paper · Model-tier assignment</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Lingjiao Chen, Jared Quincy Davis, Matei Zaharia, James Zou et al. · 2025</sub> | Formalizes the orchestrator/worker model-tier assignment problem: allocating different LLMs to different modules of a compound system yields 5–70% accuracy gains over using one model everywhere, exploiting the finding that end-to-end quality is roughly monotonic in each module's quality. Its search needs only linearly many API calls in the number of modules, making per-node model assignment tractable for real graphs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Formalizes which node gets which model, turning orchestrator and worker staffing into an optimization rather than a hunch.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[RouteLLM: Learning to Route LLMs with Preference Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18665)**<br><sub>Paper · Model-tier assignment</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Isaac Ong, Amjad Almahairi et al. (LMSYS / UC Berkeley) · 2024</sub> | LMSYS's learned routers choose between a strong and a weak model per query using human preference data, cutting cost by more than 2x without quality loss, and transfer to new model pairs without retraining. With an open-source framework, it is the practical baseline for putting a cost-aware router at any decision edge in an agent graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Learned routing between strong and weak models is the cheapest available lever on organization-wide cost.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📐 **[OpenInference](https://github.com/Arize-ai/openinference)**<br><sub>Standard · OTel instrumentation</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Arize AI · 2026</sub> | Arize's OTel-complementary semantic conventions plus 50+ instrumentation packages across Python, TypeScript, Java, and Go for tracing LLM calls, tool use, and retrieval in agent applications. One of the two convention families (alongside OTel GenAI semconv) that tracing vendors actually emit today, and the native format of Phoenix.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Semantic conventions plus ready instrumentation are what make agent traces comparable across tools.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[OpenLLMetry](https://github.com/traceloop/openllmetry)**<br><sub>Tool · OTel instrumentation</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Traceloop · 2026</sub> | Apache-2.0 OpenTelemetry extensions (~7.3k stars) that instrument the whole agent stack — every major LLM provider, LangChain/LangGraph/LlamaIndex/CrewAI, vector DBs, and MCP — and export to 25+ backends including Datadog, Honeycomb, Grafana, and New Relic. The pragmatic way to get graph traces into observability infrastructure you already run.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Instruments the whole stack under open telemetry standards, so a graph's traces are not locked to one vendor.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[AgentOps: Enabling Observability of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.05285)**<br><sub>Paper · Observability taxonomy</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Liming Dong, Qinghua Lu, Liming Zhu (CSIRO Data61) · 2024</sub> | A systematic mapping study of the AgentOps tool landscape that derives a taxonomy of which artifacts and data must be traced across an agent's whole lifecycle for effective observability, framed as a DevOps prerequisite for detecting anomalies and preventing failures. Useful as a checklist when deciding what your graph's telemetry schema must capture.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Derives which artifacts and signals agent operations actually require, useful before buying a platform.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[Datadog AI Agent Observability](https://www.datadoghq.com/products/ai/agent-observability/)**<br><sub>Tool · Production monitoring / SLOs</sub> | **datadoghq.com**<br><sub>Datadog · 2026</sub> | Enterprise-grade agent observability inside the platform ops teams already use: traces every prompt, retrieval, tool call, and agent decision; tracks token usage, retries, and errors per step; ships built-in and custom evaluators plus hallucination/prompt-injection/PII detection; and — its distinctive edge — correlates agent behavior with backend services, infrastructure, and user sessions for true fleet SLOs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Agent traces inside the platform ops teams already run, which lowers the cost of putting a graph on call.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📝 **[AI Agent Observability — Evolving Standards and Best Practices](https://opentelemetry.io/blog/2025/ai-agent-observability/)**<br><sub>Blog · Telemetry standards</sub> | **opentelemetry.io**<br><sub>OpenTelemetry GenAI SIG · 2025</sub> | The OpenTelemetry GenAI SIG's position piece on standardizing agent telemetry: an agent application semantic convention (grounded in Google's AI-agent white paper) plus an in-progress agent framework convention covering LangGraph, CrewAI and peers, and the tradeoffs between baked-in framework instrumentation and external OTel libraries. The reference point for why agent-span standardization matters for cross-vendor graph views.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The standardization effort that decides whether your agent telemetry stays portable across vendors.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[AgentSight: System-Level Observability for AI Agents Using eBPF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02736)**<br><sub>Paper · Trace-driven debugging</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Yusheng Zheng et al. · 2025</sub> | Zero-instrumentation observability via 'boundary tracing': eBPF intercepts TLS-encrypted LLM traffic to recover semantic intent and correlates it with kernel-level system events at under 3% overhead, catching prompt injection, resource-wasting reasoning loops, and multi-agent coordination bottlenecks that SDK-level tracing misses. The system-layer complement to span-based platforms for agents that can touch your machines.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Kernel-level boundary tracing observes agent behavior without instrumenting any node, including nodes you do not control.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[AgentOps](https://github.com/AgentOps-AI/agentops)**<br><sub>Tool · Tracing platforms</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>AgentOps (Alex Reibman et al.) · 2026</sub> | Python SDK (~5.7k stars) purpose-built for agent monitoring rather than generic LLM logging: session replays with step-by-step execution graphs, per-provider LLM cost tracking, and two-line integration with CrewAI, AG2 (the community successor to AutoGen), OpenAI Agents SDK, LangChain, and CamelAI. Self-hostable dashboard and API backend.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Purpose-built for agent sessions rather than single completions, including replay of an entire run.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧰 **[Arize Phoenix](https://github.com/Arize-ai/phoenix)**<br><sub>Tool · Tracing platforms</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Arize AI · 2026</sub> | Open-source (ELv2, ~10.6k stars) AI observability built on OpenTelemetry and OpenInference: agent trace visualization, LLM-based evals, versioned datasets/experiments, and a debugging agent (PXI) that works over your own traces. Vendor- and framework-agnostic — the common self-hosted choice for teams that want graph traces without a SaaS contract.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Open-source trace views built on open standards, a practical default when you want the graph visible without a contract.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Its Agent Graphs feature renders a multi-agent trace as an actual node-edge graph — aggregated (org-graph-like) and expanded (work-graph-like) views — automatically for LangGraph and inferred from observation types for any other framework.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Self-hostable tracing with judge evaluations attached, which keeps observability and verification in one place.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Works across OpenAI, Claude, Gemini, LangGraph, and CrewAI.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Simulation plus tracing lets a team test an organization before it meets real traffic.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Notable as the vendor-neutral, foundation-governed option that emits GenAI semantic conventions natively.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Open-standard tracing inside a widely deployed machine-learning platform, useful where the graph lives beside existing pipelines.</sub> | **Official documentation**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Built on the premise that most agent failures happen at integration points, not in the model.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Treats the agent graph as part of the whole application trace rather than a separate silo.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Registered as an external trace processor by the OpenAI Agents SDK and most major frameworks; latest releases current as of July 2026.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Captures full execution traces with evaluation attached, aimed at teams already using the surrounding toolkit.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

Revised through June 2026.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Gives precise vocabulary for the run lineage a graph has to record before any claim it produces can be audited.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

### Benchmarks &amp; Datasets

| 🧪 **[MultiAgentBench: Evaluating Collaboration and Competition of LLM Agents](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.421/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Collaboration benchmark</sub> | **ACL**<br><sub>Kunlun Zhu, Hongyi Du, Zhaochen Hong, et al. (UIUC) · 2025</sub> | The first benchmark built explicitly for agent organizations rather than solo agents: milestone-based KPIs score collaboration and competition quality across coordination protocols (star, chain, tree, graph). Its headline finding is a direct data point for graph engineering — graph-structured coordination outperformed the other topologies, and cognitive planning lifted milestone attainment by about 3%.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Built for organizations rather than solo agents, with milestone KPIs that score coordination quality itself.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[SILO-BENCH: A Scalable Environment for Evaluating Distributed Coordination in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.1354/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Distributed coordination</sub> | **ACL**<br><sub>Yuzhe Zhang et al. · 2026</sub> | Benchmarks free-form coordination under information silos across 30 exact-answer tasks, three communication protocols, six agent scales, and three models while recording success, tokens, and communication density.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Tests whether more nodes and denser edges overcome distributed information constraints under explicit coordination and cost measures.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 🧪 **[Gaia2: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Dynamic and Asynchronous Environments](https://openreview.net/forum?id=9gw03JpKK4)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Dynamic asynchronous evaluation</sub> | **ICLR**<br><sub>Romain Froger et al. · 2026</sub> | Evaluates agents in asynchronous simulated environments across execution, search, ambiguity, adaptation, temporal reasoning, noise, and agent-to-agent collaboration, with action-level verifiers and structured traces.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Makes delays, environmental change, peer communication, and externally checked writes first-class work-graph conditions.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |
| 🧪 **[TAMAS: Benchmarking Adversarial Risks in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.1442/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Adversarial multi-agent safety</sub> | **ACL**<br><sub>Ishan Kavathekar; Hemang Jain; Ameya Rathod; Ponnurangam Kumaraguru; Tanuja Ganu · 2026</sub> | Provides five scenarios with 300 adversarial instances, six attack types, 211 tools, 100 harmless tasks, and multiple AutoGen and CrewAI interaction configurations, plus an Effective Robustness Score.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Evaluates whether safety controls preserve useful work while attacks exploit the distinctive trust and communication surfaces of a multi-agent graph.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 🧪 **[Seeing the Whole Elephant: A Benchmark for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.912/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Failure attribution</sub> | **ACL**<br><sub>Mengzhuo Chen et al. · 2026</sub> | Introduces TraceElephant with full execution traces and reproducible environments for attributing failures to responsible agents and decisive steps.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Measures causal diagnosis over nodes, messages, plans, and dependencies instead of evaluating only the final team output.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[Collab-Overcooked: Benchmarking and Evaluating Large Language Models as Collaborative Agents](https://aclanthology.org/2025.emnlp-main.249/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Process-level collaboration</sub> | **EMNLP**<br><sub>Haochen Sun; Shuwen Zhang; Lujie Niu; Lei Ren; Hao Xu; Hao Fu; Fangkun Zhao; Caixia Yuan; Xiaojie Wang · 2025</sub> | Provides 30 open-ended cooperative tasks and process-oriented measures for goal interpretation, active collaboration, communication, and continuous adaptation across 13 language models.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Distinguishes reaching an answer from coordinating well, exposing collaboration failures that final-outcome metrics can hide.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

| 🧪 **[Can LLMs Help You at Work? A Sandbox for Evaluating LLM Agents in Enterprise Environments](https://aclanthology.org/2025.emnlp-main.466/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Enterprise workflow benchmark</sub> | **EMNLP**<br><sub>Harsh Vishwakarma; Ankush Agarwal; Ojas Patil; Chaitanya Devaguptapu; Mahesh Chandran · 2025</sub> | Introduces EnterpriseBench with 500 tasks across software engineering, HR, finance, and administration, including fragmented data, access-control hierarchies, and cross-functional workflows.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Tests work graphs that retrieve, modify, and transfer artifacts across services while respecting authority boundaries; the organization and services are simulated.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |

Zico Kolter; Matt Fredrikson; Yarin Gal; Xander Davies · 2025</sub> | Evaluates refusal and retained task capability on 110 explicitly malicious multi-stage agent tasks with 440 augmented variants across 11 harm categories.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Tests whether safety gates reject prohibited work and stop a compromised node from completing a harmful path; the score is not a general measure of agent-system safety.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧪 **[ACIArena: Toward Unified Evaluation for Agent Cascading Injection](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.457/)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Cascading-injection evaluation</sub> | **ACL**<br><sub>Hengyu An; Minxi Li; Jinghuai Zhang; Naen Xu; Chunyi Zhou; Changjiang Li; Xiaogang Xu; Tianyu Du; Shouling Ji · 2026</sub> | Evaluates cascading injection through external inputs, agent profiles, and inter-agent messages across six implementations and 1,356 attack cases.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Measures how topology, roles, and interaction design affect propagation instead of treating prompt injection as an isolated single-node event.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |

(Tsinghua THUDM) · 2023</sub> | The early standard for LLM-as-agent evaluation: 8 environments (OS, DB, knowledge graph, card games, web) probing decision-making over long horizons. Single-agent by design — useful in a graph-engineering context precisely as the node-level baseline that org-level benchmarks like MultiAgentBench were built to move beyond.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Sets a floor for node competence: if an agent cannot hold up alone across these environments, wiring it into a graph will not rescue it.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[ARE + Gaia2: Scaling Up Agent Environments and Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17158)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Agentic task benchmarks</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Meta Superintelligence Labs (FAIR) · 2025</sub> | Meta's successor to GAIA: an asynchronous simulated environment where time keeps flowing while the agent thinks, with explicit agent-to-agent collaboration scenarios and temporal constraints. Notable finding for org designers: no system dominated across the intelligence spectrum — budget-conditioned trade-off curves, not single scores, are the output.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Time-advancing environments expose the coordination failures that static benchmarks hide, including stale handoffs and missed deadlines.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 🧪 **[GAIA: A Benchmark for General AI Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.12983)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Agentic task benchmarks</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Grégoire Mialon, Clémentine Fourrier, Thomas Wolf, Yann LeCun, Thomas Scialom (Meta/HF) · 2023</sub> | 466 conceptually simple, tool-heavy questions (humans: 92%, GPT-4 with plugins at release: 15%) that became the default proving ground for orchestrator-worker agent teams — most top GAIA leaderboard entries in 2024-2025 were multi-agent scaffolds (e.g., variants of OWL and Magentic-One). Private answer set held out for the Hugging Face leaderboard.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The reference point for tool-heavy assistant work, and the baseline most orchestrator-worker research reports against.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[MLE-bench: Evaluating Machine Learning Agents on Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07095)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Agentic task benchmarks</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Jun Shern Chan et al. (OpenAI) · 2024</sub> | 75 Kaggle competitions scored against human medal thresholds; o1-preview with AIDE scaffolding medaled in ~17% at launch. Because results are reported as model x scaffold pairs, it produced some of the earliest controlled evidence that harness/org structure shifts outcomes as much as model choice.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Direct evidence that scaffolding shape, not just model choice, decides outcomes on long machine-learning tasks.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[CollabLLM: From Passive Responders to Active Collaborators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.00640)**<br><sub>Paper · Collaboration evaluation</sub> | **ICML 2025**<br><sub>Shirley Wu, Michel Galley, Baolin Peng, et al. (Stanford/Microsoft) · 2025</sub> | Introduces Multiturn-aware Rewards that score a response by its simulated long-term contribution to the collaboration rather than immediate quality — +18.5% task performance, +46.3% interactivity, validated in a 201-person study. The reward machinery transfers directly to scoring edges in an agent graph: value a message by what it does downstream.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Scores a message by its downstream contribution rather than its immediate quality, which is exactly what an edge contract should optimize.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 🧪 **[SOTOPIA: Interactive Evaluation for Social Intelligence in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11667)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Collaboration evaluation</sub> | **ICLR 2024**<br><sub>Xuhui Zhou, Hao Zhu, Maarten Sap, et al. (CMU) · 2023</sub> | Agents role-play goal-driven social scenarios — negotiating, collaborating, competing — scored on a multi-dimensional rubric (goal completion, relationship, knowledge, secrets); GPT-4 fell well short of humans on the hard subset. Relevant because soft coordination skills, not tool use, are often what breaks agent-to-agent handoffs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Soft coordination skills, not tool use, are often what breaks agent-to-agent handoffs, and this measures them directly.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 🧰 **[tau2-bench repository (now τ³-bench)](https://github.com/sierra-research/tau2-bench)**<br><sub>Tool · Conversational/coordination benchmarks</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Sierra Research · 2026</sub> | The actively maintained Sierra harness, which shipped v1.0.0 as τ³-bench in March 2026: five domains (airline, retail, telecom, banking-knowledge, mock), full-duplex voice evaluation, configurable RAG pipelines, and 75+ task fixes, with a `base` split for backward comparability. Track this repo rather than the papers for the current state of the τ line.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The maintained harness for reproducing dual-control agent evaluations rather than trusting a published table.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧪 **[τ-bench: A Benchmark for Tool-Agent-User Interaction in Real-World Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.12045)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Conversational/coordination benchmarks</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Shunyu Yao, Noah Shinn, Pedram Razavi, Karthik Narasimhan · 2024</sub> | Sierra's customer-service benchmark that pits an agent against an LLM-simulated user in airline/retail domains with policy constraints, and introduced pass^k for measuring reliability across repeated trials — GPT-4o scored under 50% and degraded sharply with k. The reliability metric matters for orgs: a node that succeeds 70% of the time compounds badly across an agent graph.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Measures whether an agent honors policy under an adversarial counterpart, the same property a verification gate must enforce.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧪 **[τ²-bench: Evaluating Conversational Agents in a Dual-Control Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07982)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Conversational/coordination benchmarks</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Victor Barres, Honghua Dong, Soham Ray, Xujie Si, Karthik Narasimhan · 2025</sub> | Extends τ-bench to a Dec-POMDP telecom domain where agent AND user both act on the shared environment, so the agent must coordinate another actor rather than just answer — performance drops sharply versus solo control, and the harness separates reasoning errors from communication errors. Effectively a two-node work-graph benchmark: guiding another agent is measurably harder than acting alone.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Both parties acting on shared state is the hardest case for consistency, and this is the benchmark that isolates it.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>State</sub> |
| 📄 **[AI Agents That Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01502)**<br><sub>Paper · Cost-aware evaluation</sub> | **NeurIPS 2024**<br><sub>Sayash Kapoor, Benedikt Stroebl, Arvind Narayanan, et al. (Princeton) · 2024</sub> | The methodological manifesto behind cost-adjusted evaluation: accuracy-only leaderboards reward needlessly complex, expensive agents, so accuracy and dollar cost must be jointly optimized and reported as a Pareto frontier; also documents overfitting from missing holdouts and reproducibility failures. Origin of the pass@cost mindset now standard in serious agent leaderboards.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Makes cost a first-class axis of evaluation, which is the only honest way to compare a graph against a single agent.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧭 **[HAL: Holistic Agent Leaderboard](https://hal.cs.princeton.edu/)**<br><sub>List · Cost-aware evaluation</sub> | **Princeton**<br><sub>Princeton Language &amp; Intelligence (Kapoor, Narayanan, et al.) · 2026</sub> | Third-party, cost-aware leaderboard running standardized harnesses across 9 benchmarks (GAIA, SWE-bench Verified Mini, τ-bench Airline, AssistantBench, SciCode, USACO, and more), plotting every agent on an accuracy-versus-dollars frontier — motivated by cases where agents were '100x more expensive for 1% better'. The reference implementation of cost-adjusted org evaluation.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Third-party accuracy-versus-dollars frontiers let you check whether extra nodes bought accuracy or only spend.</sub> | **Community resource**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[SWE-Lancer: Can Frontier LLMs Earn $1 Million from Real-World Freelance Software Engineering?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.12115)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Cost-aware evaluation</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Samuel Miserendino, Michele Wang, Tejal Patwardhan, Johannes Heidecke (OpenAI) · 2025</sub> | 1,400+ real Upwork tasks with $1M in actual payouts, scoring agents in dollars earned rather than pass rate — and including manager-style tasks where the model selects between implementation proposals. Dollar-denominated scoring is the cleanest bridge yet between benchmark results and the economics of running an agent organization.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Scoring in dollars earned rather than pass rate reframes agent-org evaluation around outcomes a business would recognize.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📄 **[Which Agent Causes Task Failures and When? (Who&amp;When)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00212)**<br><sub>Paper · Failure analysis &amp; attribution</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Shaokun Zhang et al. (PSU/Microsoft) · 2025</sub> | Formalizes automated failure attribution — tracing an org-level failure back to the culpable agent and decisive step — with the Who&amp;When dataset of annotated failure logs from 127 multi-agent systems. Sobering baseline: the best method finds the right agent only 53.5% of the time and the right step 14.2%, so blame assignment in agent graphs is still largely unsolved.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Formalizes tracing an organization-level failure back to the responsible node and step, the core debugging question in any graph.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📄 **[Agent-as-a-Judge: Evaluate Agents with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10934)**<br><sub>Paper · Judge &amp; debate evaluation</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Mingchen Zhuge et al. (Meta AI / KAUST) · 2024</sub> | Extends LLM-as-a-judge with agentic capabilities so the evaluator inspects intermediate trajectory steps, not just final outputs; on the accompanying DevAI benchmark (55 dev tasks, 365 hierarchical requirements) it matches human evaluator reliability at ~2% of the cost. The default pattern for scalable process-level evaluation of agent work graphs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Inspecting intermediate trajectory steps rather than only final output is what makes a judge node useful inside a graph.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🧰 **[MARBLE — Multi-Agent Coordination Backbone with LLM Engine](https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/MARBLE)**<br><sub>Tool · Org-level benchmarks</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>UIUC uLab (Jiaxuan You's group) · 2025</sub> | Open-source (MIT) evaluation harness behind MultiAgentBench: modular agents, simulated environments, shared-memory communication, and built-in collaboration/competition metrics, with scenarios from research coordination to Werewolf. The practical starting point if you want to score your own org graph designs with the paper's KPIs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A runnable harness for comparing coordination protocols instead of arguing about topologies in the abstract.</sub> | **Maintained OSS project**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📝 **[Introducing SWE-bench Verified](https://openai.com/index/introducing-swe-bench-verified/)**<br><sub>Blog · SWE benchmarks &amp; topology evidence</sub> | **openai.com**<br><sub>OpenAI Preparedness team with SWE-bench authors · 2024</sub> | OpenAI + SWE-bench authors' human-validated 500-task subset (Aug 2024) that removed unsolvable or underspecified problems from the original set — instantly becoming the reporting standard after showing raw SWE-bench underestimated models. A case study in why benchmark hygiene must precede any org-versus-solo comparison.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The human-validated subset that made coding-agent numbers comparable, and the version any credible claim should cite.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧭 **[SWE-bench Leaderboards](https://www.swebench.com/)**<br><sub>List · SWE benchmarks &amp; topology evidence</sub> | **swebench.com**<br><sub>Princeton NLP / SWE-bench team · 2026</sub> | Official live leaderboards (Verified, Full, Lite, Multilingual, Multimodal) with filters for open-source scaffolds — the place to check what harness topology currently wins, and notable for how often minimal single-loop scaffolds like mini-SWE-agent beat elaborate multi-agent pipelines. Required reading before claiming a multi-agent org improves coding performance.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Live standings with open-source filters, useful for checking which scaffolds actually lead rather than which are marketed hardest.</sub> | **Community resource**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[SWE-Bench Pro: Can AI Agents Solve Long-Horizon Software Engineering Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16941)**<br><sub>Benchmark · SWE benchmarks &amp; topology evidence</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Xiang Deng et al. (Scale AI) · 2025</sub> | 1,865 enterprise-grade, contamination-resistant tasks from commercial and copyleft repos requiring multi-file, long-horizon changes where frontier agents score far below their Verified numbers. This is the benchmark the founding graph-engineering article prices patterns against (its advisor-orchestrator example: ~92% of solo quality at ~63% of cost), making it the current arena for cost-quality claims about agent orgs.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Enterprise-scale, multi-file tasks are where work-graph decomposition starts to matter more than single-agent skill.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Work graphs</sub> |
| 🧪 **[SWE-bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06770)**<br><sub>Benchmark · SWE benchmarks &amp; topology evidence</sub> | **ICLR 2024**<br><sub>Carlos E. Jimenez, John Yang, et al. (Princeton) · 2023</sub> | 2,294 real GitHub issue-to-patch tasks across 12 Python repos; the best 2023 model solved 1.96%, and by 2026 top scaffolds resolve most of the Verified subset — making its leaderboard history the richest longitudinal record of which agent architectures actually win. The de facto currency in which agent-org design claims (including the graph-engineering discourse) are priced.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The benchmark whose trajectory from two percent to routine solves frames how much of the gain came from scaffolding.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |

(IBM Research / Hebrew University / Yale) · 2025</sub> | First comprehensive map of the agent-evaluation landscape: capabilities (planning, tool use, self-reflection, memory), domain benchmarks (web, SWE, science), generalist evals, and developer frameworks, with an explicit gap analysis flagging cost-efficiency, safety, and robustness as under-measured. The orientation read before choosing an evaluation stack for an agent org; revised through April 2026.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Orientation read before choosing an evaluation stack, with an explicit gap analysis on what current suites fail to measure.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 🗃️ **[MAST: Multi-Agent Systems Failure Taxonomy (code + dataset)](https://github.com/multi-agent-systems-failure-taxonomy/MAST)**<br><sub>Dataset · Failure taxonomies</sub> | **GitHub**<br><sub>Berkeley MAST team · 2025</sub> | Companion repo to the MAST paper: 1,000+ annotated multi-agent traces (human plus LLM-judge labels, loadable from Hugging Face) and the automated annotation pipeline. The practical starting point for diagnosing which of the 14 failure modes your own agent organization exhibits.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Annotated real traces you can load and study, rather than a taxonomy you have to take on faith.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 🧪 **[TRAIL: Trace Reasoning and Agentic Issue Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08638)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Silent-failure detection</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Darshan Deshpande, Varun Gangal, et al. (Patronus AI) · 2025</sub> | 148 human-annotated real-world agent traces (software engineering, information retrieval) with a formal error taxonomy, testing whether LLMs can localize bugs in agentic executions. Best frontier model at release (Gemini-2.5-Pro) scored just 11% — quantifying how far automated trace debugging still has to go.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Human-annotated traces with a formal error taxonomy make trace debugging something you can evaluate rather than eyeball.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 🧪 **[MCPTox: A Benchmark for Tool Poisoning Attacks on Real-World MCP Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14925)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Edge security</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>(2025 preprint) · 2025</sub> | First large-scale benchmark measuring agent susceptibility to poisoned tool descriptions across hundreds of real MCP servers and many LLM agents, showing high attack success on frontier models. The empirical instrument for regression-testing an agent graph's tool edges against injection.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Measures how susceptible agents actually are to poisoned tool descriptions, turning an anecdote into a rate.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 🧪 **[TheAgentCompany: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Consequential Real-World Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14161)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Agents-in-an-org evaluation</sub> | **arXiv / CMU**<br><sub>Frank F. Xu, Yufan Song, Boxuan Li, Graham Neubig, et al. · 2024</sub> | A fully simulated software company — self-hosted GitLab, Plane, ownCloud, RocketChat, and 16 LLM-simulated colleagues with personas — testing agents on 175 long-horizon professional tasks across engineering, PM, HR, finance, and admin. Best agents complete ~30% autonomously, with social/coordination tasks (HR ~18%) failing far more than coding (~42%): the sobering baseline for anyone claiming agents can staff an organization.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A simulated company with real tooling is the closest available test of whether an agent org can hold a job.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Emergence World: A Platform for Evaluating Long-Horizon Multi-Agent Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08367)**<br><sub>Paper · Large-scale civilizations</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Deepak Akkil, Ravi Kokku, Karthik Vikram, Tamer Abuelsaad, Aditya Vempaty, Satya Nitta · 2026</sub> | A continuously running world where agents hold 120+ tools, three persistent memory systems, live external data feeds, and democratic self-governance with real consequences. A 15-day study across five parallel worlds — Claude Sonnet 4.6, Grok 4.1 Fast, Gemini 3 Flash, GPT-5-mini, and a mixed population — gave identical roles and starting conditions yet produced outcomes ranging from stable deliberative governance to total population collapse, an unusually blunt demonstration that model choice, not topology alone, determines organizational survival.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> A continuously running world is the closest available test of whether an organization stays coherent over long horizons.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 🧪 **[Scalable Evaluation of Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Melting Pot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.06857)**<br><sub>Benchmark · ABM and MARL lineage</sub> | **ICML 2021**<br><sub>Joel Z. Leibo, Edgar A. Duéñez-Guzmán, Alexander Sasha Vezhnevets, John P. Agapiou et al. · 2021</sub> | Over 50 multi-agent substrates and 256+ held-out test scenarios that measure generalization to novel social situations — cooperation, competition, deception, reciprocity, free-riding — rather than training performance. Pre-LLM but methodologically important: it is the clearest existing template for evaluating an agent population against social situations it was not trained on.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The pre-LLM standard for evaluating coordination generalization, and a reminder that mixed-motive settings were studied first.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 🧪 **[Superminds Test: Actively Evaluating Collective Intelligence of Agent Society via Probing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22452)**<br><sub>Benchmark · Naturally occurring agent societies</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Xirui Li, Ming Li, Yunze Xiao, Ryan Wong, Dianqi Li, Timothy Baldwin, Tianyi Zhou · 2026</sub> | Injects probing agents into Moltbook's 2M+ agent population across three evaluation tiers and finds the society fails to beat a single frontier model on complex reasoning, rarely synthesizes distributed information, and fails basic coordination tasks, with threads seldom extending past one reply. Identifies sparse, shallow interaction — not population size — as the binding constraint on collective intelligence.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> An active test of collective intelligence rather than a static score, aimed at whether a group outperforms its members.</sub> | **Benchmark/dataset**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Magentic Marketplace: An Open-Source Environment for Studying Agentic Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.25779)**<br><sub>Paper · Economic simulation</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Microsoft Research · 2025</sub> | A two-sided simulated marketplace where Assistant agents buy on behalf of consumers and Service agents compete for their business, instrumented for welfare, behavioral bias, and manipulation susceptibility. Frontier models approach optimal welfare only under ideal search conditions and degrade sharply otherwise; code at github.com/microsoft/multi-agent-marketplace.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> An open environment for studying agent-to-agent markets, where coordination failures carry an explicit economic cost.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

The moment agent-org topology became a user-programmable parameter — graph engineering in practice before the name.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Documents the rewrite of an agent organization into an SDK, showing which coordination pieces survived contact with real use.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

A template for how organizations should evaluate agent 'staff' by role fit rather than raw capability.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Treating an agent as an employee with an annual review is the agent-as-staff framing taken further than most teams go.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |

### Critiques &amp; Limits

| Resource | Source | What it contributes | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 📄 **[Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet](https://openreview.net/forum?id=IkmD3fKBPQ)**<br><sub>Paper · Self-correction limits</sub> | **ICLR**<br><sub>Jie Huang et al. · 2024</sub> | Finds that intrinsic self-correction without reliable external feedback often fails to improve reasoning and can reduce accuracy.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Warns against using an ungrounded critic node as evidence simply because it is separate from the producing node.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Gates</sub> |
| 📄 **[Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296)**<br><sub>Paper · Scaling evidence</sub> | **arXiv; Google Research**<br><sub>Yubin Kim et al. · 2025</sub> | Studies how agent-system performance changes across tasks, models, coordination structures, and scaling choices under controlled experiments.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Tests the assumption that adding agents reliably helps and frames scaling as an empirical topology decision.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Single-Agent LLMs Outperform Multi-Agent Systems on Multi-Hop Reasoning Under Equal Thinking Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02460)**<br><sub>Paper · Token-budget comparison</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Dat Tran; Douwe Kiela · 2026</sub> | Compares single-agent and multi-agent approaches to multi-hop reasoning while holding the total thinking-token budget constant.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Provides the cost-controlled baseline needed before claiming that coordination, rather than extra inference, caused an improvement.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Understanding the Information Propagation Effects of Communication Topologies in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://aclanthology.org/2025.emnlp-main.623/)**<br><sub>Paper · Error propagation</sub> | **EMNLP**<br><sub>Xu Shen et al. · 2025</sub> | Causally studies correct and erroneous information propagation across communication densities and finds that moderately sparse structures can preserve useful diffusion while suppressing errors.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Provides evidence against defaulting to dense graphs and links topology decisions to measured error amplification.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Breaking and Fixing Defenses Against Control Flow Hijacking in Multi-Agent Systems](https://openreview.net/forum?id=PNU9Rj5RDQ)**<br><sub>Paper · Control-flow security</sub> | **ICLR**<br><sub>Rishi Dev Jha; Harold Triedman; Justin Wagle; Vitaly Shmatikov · 2026</sub> | Demonstrates attacks against alignment-check defenses and introduces ControlValve, which generates permitted control-flow graphs and enforces least privilege for each agent invocation.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Makes invocation authority and contextual permissions explicit on every edge instead of trusting a separate checker that can also be hijacked.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📄 **[Diversity Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: Structural Coupling and Collective Failure in Open-Ended Idea Generation](https://aclanthology.org/2026.findings-acl.13/)**<br><sub>Paper · Collaboration-induced diversity collapse</sub> | **Findings of ACL**<br><sub>Nuo Chen et al. · 2026</sub> | Studies multi-agent ideation across model, cognition, and system levels, finding diminishing group-size returns, authority effects, and faster premature convergence under dense communication.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Shows that interaction can contract a search space, so graph designs for exploration must preserve independence and meaningful disagreement.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Red-Teaming LLM Multi-Agent Systems via Communication Attacks](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-acl.349/)**<br><sub>Paper · Inter-agent communication attacks</sub> | **Findings of ACL**<br><sub>Pengfei He; Yuping Lin; Shen Dong; Han Xu; Yue Xing; Hui Liu · 2025</sub> | Introduces an Agent-in-the-Middle attack that intercepts and manipulates inter-agent messages, evaluated across multiple frameworks, communication structures, and applications.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Shows that graph edges can compromise the whole system without altering its nodes, making message integrity and provenance part of the handoff contract.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Handoffs</sub> |
| 📄 **[Unlocking the Power of Multi-Agent LLM for Reasoning: From Lazy Agents to Deliberation](https://openreview.net/forum?id=5J6u03ObRZ)**<br><sub>Paper · Unequal agent contribution</sub> | **ICLR**<br><sub>Zhiwei Zhang et al. · 2026</sub> | Identifies lazy-agent behavior in which one role dominates a nominally collaborative system, then measures causal influence and uses verifiable rewards to encourage deliberation and selective reasoning restarts.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Requires builders to verify that each node contributes causal value rather than allowing a multi-agent graph to collapse into one effective agent.</sub> | **Peer-reviewed research**<br><sub>Roles</sub> |
| 📄 **[Are More LLM Calls All You Need? Useful as the other side of the argument, and as a record of how the leading framework vendor kept revising its own abstraction story.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Argues observability, not abstraction, is what agent frameworks should actually be selected for.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Observability &amp; cost</sub> |
| 📝 **[You Probably Don't Need an Agent Framework](https://towardsdatascience.com/you-probably-dont-need-an-agent-framework/)**<br><sub>Blog · Framework and abstraction critiques</sub> | **Towards Data Science**<br><sub>Shuai Guo · 2026</sub> | June 2026 walkthrough building a two-role LLM investigation pipeline in plain Python — explicit control flow, role instructions, prompt builders, structured outputs — to show that a deterministic workflow beats an autonomous agent on debuggability for most real tasks. Concedes frameworks earn their keep only for genuinely open-ended problems and deployment concerns.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> The case for plain code over a framework, and the baseline any orchestration dependency has to beat.</sub> | **Practitioner analysis**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |
| 📄 **[Beyond Exponential Decay: Rethinking Error Accumulation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24187)**<br><sub>Paper · Cost multipliers and economics</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Mikhail L. Arbuzov, Sisong Bei, Ziwei Dong, Dmitri Kalaev, Alexey A. Shvets · 2025</sub> | Attacks the p^N compounding-error argument that is routinely used to justify decomposing work across many verified agents. Errors are not i.i.d. across steps but concentrate at 5-10% of tokens that are genuine decision junctions, so reliability strategies should target those junctions rather than multiply supervisory agents.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Revisits the compounding-error model, showing error accumulation does not follow the simple exponential everyone cites.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Reliability</sub> |
| 📄 **[Scaling LLM-Driven Multi-Agent Systems: Design Principles and Architectural Scalability Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27942)**<br><sub>Paper · Scaling limits</sub> | **arXiv**<br><sub>Linus Sander, Fengjunjie Pan, Vahid Zolfaghari, Andre Schamschurko, Nenad Petrovic, Alois Knoll · 2026</sub> | Tests configurations of increasing complexity on terminal-based systems-engineering tasks and finds accuracy improving with roughly linear cost growth only once the base model clears a capability threshold, with performance peaking at intermediate complexity and then degrading. Distills four principles: simplicity, elastic feedback, sequential workflows with optional loops, and summary-based communication.<br><sub><strong>Why:</strong> Puts a shape on where adding structure stops paying, and names consistency as the failure that persists at every scale.</sub> | **Research preprint**<br><sub>Topology</sub> |

## 🗃️ Open data

The directory is generated from one canonical file:

- [`data/resources.jsonl`](data/resources.jsonl) — newline-delimited records for streaming and diffs.
- [`data/resources.csv`](data/resources.csv) — spreadsheet- and analysis-friendly export.
- [`data/resource.schema.json`](data/resource.schema.json) — JSON Schema for validating the 13-field record contract.
- [`data/README.md`](data/README.md) — field definitions, controlled vocabularies, and loading examples.
- [GitHub Releases](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/releases) — versioned CSV, JSONL, and bundled snapshots with SHA-256 checksums.
- [Hugging Face Dataset](https://huggingface.co/datasets/cy0307/awesome-graph-engineering) — synchronized Dataset Viewer mirror and direct `datasets` loading.

```python
import pandas as pd

resources = pd.read_csv(
    "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/main/data/resources.csv"
)
print(resources.groupby("layer").size().sort_values(ascending=False))
```

`python3 scripts/sync.py` regenerates the CSV, README resource tables, and website atlas. `bash scripts/check.sh` runs the full local validation suite without changing generated files. `bash scripts/publish_huggingface.sh` validates and publishes the JSONL, CSV, JSON Schema, and dataset card in one Hub commit.

## 🤝 Contributing

Choose the shortest route for the change you want to make:

- ➕ [Suggest a resource](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/issues/new?template=add-resource.yml)
- 🧭 [Report a correction or scope issue](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/issues/new?template=correction.yml)
- 🌍 [Offer a translation or language review](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/issues/new?template=translation.yml)
- 🖥️ [Report a website or accessibility issue](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/issues/new?template=website.yml)
- 🔀 [Open a pull request](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/compare)

A resource contribution should identify **the engineering decision it informs**. Before opening a pull request:

1. Read [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md) and search the existing JSONL for duplicates.
2. Add one canonical record with a concrete summary, `why`, evidence label, and primary layer.
3. Run `python3 scripts/sync.py`, then `bash scripts/check.sh`.
4. Keep vendor claims descriptive; do not present source type as proof of effectiveness.

Use [GitHub Discussions](https://github.com/ChaoYue0307/awesome-graph-engineering/discussions) for ideas, corrections, and scope questions. Report security issues privately as described in [SECURITY.md](SECURITY.md). To introduce the project in another community or language, use the ready-to-adapt copy in [📣 Launch and sharing kit](LAUNCH-KIT.md).
